# Unfilter digest 10 — pregnancy, birth preparation and the first weeks


2026-11-17. Scope: voice/reddit-ur09.jsonl (349 quotes), voice/reddit-ur34.jsonl (434), voice/reddit-ur38.jsonl (385): **1,168 quote records**. All were read, alongside pregnancy-birth/README.md, the current findings and each assigned ledger’s “What these posts add” section. This digest is for the final findings rewrite; it does not change the findings or the source records.


**How to read the evidence.** Pattern statements, comparisons and suggested changes are interpretation. Every labelled quotation block reproduces the entire record’s quote field, including its spelling, punctuation, internal line breaks, links and any existing trim markers. Further IDs identify supporting evidence without pretending it is another person. Money and request indexes link to full quotations already printed, or print them where first needed.


**Counts are quote counts, never people or prevalence.** These are formerly excluded, deliberately selected posts. The 1,168 IDs contain 1,167 distinct quote strings: [pbu34-0141] and [pbu38-0057] are identical text. The antibiotic crosspost material [pbu34-0141] [pbu34-0142] [pbu34-0400] [pbu38-0057] is one account for interpretation. [pbu34-0286] is contained in [pbu34-0287], so those passages are also not independent support. Other extracts from one post can describe different parts of the same experience.


**Provenance and limits.** The ledger flags a paraphrasing preamble in the source behind [pbu38-0170], and possible AI assistance in the citation-led advocacy source behind [pbu38-0349] [pbu38-0350]. Their words remain evidence of what the source says, not independent verification of physiology. Reported diagnoses, drug effects, risk figures, hospital policies, legal limits, causal explanations and religious beliefs throughout this digest are attributed accounts. No network or git was used. No subject was treated as a reason to suppress its evidence.


## Patterns


The route through the evidence is A–U: loss; history and preparation after loss; reassurance; screening/diagnosis; continuation/abortion decisions; coercion; mental health; medication; substances/recovery; physical capacity; birth/consent; positive outcomes; recovery/feeding; separation/sleep; partner capacity; relatives/privacy; care navigation; material constraints; tools/information; sex/body/trauma; and material without a developed home in the current findings.


### A. Loss and death belong inside the pregnancy-to-first-weeks story, including its physical recovery.


**Belongs in:** §2, The arc; §6, fears and needs; add a developed loss-and-bereavement section.


**Relationship to the current findings: Extends.** Current conclusion: “The first weeks include physical incapacity, feeding pressure, conflicting instructions, loneliness, regret and delayed connection.”


**What should change:** The arc must also allow miscarriage, termination, stillbirth and neonatal death rather than assuming that every pregnancy proceeds to a living newborn at home. Include the physical passage of a miscarriage, choices about management, milk after a stillbirth, waiting to learn why a baby died, and partners' direct witnessing. The experience is not uniformly excruciating: the relatively painless miscarriage account belongs beside severe pain and haemorrhage, not outside the range.


These passages include a loss after apparently reassuring care, failed attempts to prevent another loss, the death of one twin while another remains alive, and delivery at 21 weeks after a reportedly normal anatomy scan. Preparation and accounts of what can happen are explicitly requested. The mild-miscarriage account describes handling fetal tissue for testing in detail; it is retained in full.


**Verbatim evidence** (39 quote records assigned to this pattern; this is a coverage grouping, not a prevalence or independent-source count).

<a id="q-pbu09-0151"></a>


**[pbu09-0151]**

> I (39F, married, 1 LC) had a hard time finding detailed descriptions of miscarriage experiences, especially past 10 weeks, and especially for expectant/wait-and-see management for missed miscarriage (MMC). For example, only two stories from experiences of MMC here: \[https://www.miscarriageassociation.org.uk/information/your-stories/\](https://www.miscarriageassociation.org.uk/information/your-stories/) were even similar in timing to mine. And as it turns out, neither was anything similar to my physical experience of miscarrying. While these stories were helpful, almost all of them described extreme pain, emotional turmoil, lots of heavy bleeding and intense cramps culminating in passing fetal tissue, severe nausea/vomiting, etc. I was therefore very caught off guard when my miscarraige occurred less than 18 hours before my scheduled D&C with very minor symptoms overall. I will go into graphic detail and timeline below from initial diagnosis (NIPT +T21 at 11w2d) to today (3 days after physical miscarraige at 13w). Hoping my story helps someone else looking for data points/varied experiences; and, frankly, it is helping me to write it out and reflect on the roller coaster of the last few weeks.


<a id="q-pbu09-0152"></a>


**[pbu09-0152]**

> When we got home, I went to bathroom to clean up and expected a lot of blood, but instead what I saw in the pad was the fetus and a few small clots and lots of amniotic fluid. So, it was actually my water breaking (which makes total sense comparing to my previous experience). I couldn't beliebe that I was seeing the fetus. Originally when I thought I would need to TFMR, I preferred medical because I wanted to see it, but as more time passed, I couldn't help but be horrified that it was in  my body for three weeks and I did not think I wanted to see it nor did I think I would! Most of the experiences I read said they passed it directly into toilet during very strong cramps and heavy bleeding. I never had heavy bleeding. Immediately after passing the fetus, the cramps went away and I felt much better. I asked my husband to keep it for genetic testing and also the sight of it was making me want to throw up. He wrapped the whole pad in paper towels and stuck it in a ziploc in the bathroom sink. I said we needed to get it off the paper/pad so it would be testable but he couldn't handle it. After several minutes to collect myself, I was able to brush it off with a Q-Tip so that only the fetus was in the bag. It was horrific, it was gray-brown with dark eye holes, approximately 1.25" long. This was by far the worst part of the whole experience, just the shock of it all.


<a id="q-pbu09-0153"></a>


**[pbu09-0153]**

> So, I'm sorry (or you're welcome) for this very long post, but I felt it was important to share that MMC & miscarriage in general doesn't always have to be painful. I had a pain-free (uncomfortable) birth previously and I expected to lean into it when miscarriage occurred. Every body is different, and not everyone has this type of experience, but surely some must? I can't be the only one, and after my first birth, I was hoping I could cope and handle the miscarriage similarly and I think mentally I did. The nursing staff looked at me like I was crazy when I mentioned my prior experience and asked. She fear mongered that it was going to be extremely painful, just like society does for labor/delivery. For me, personally, I just wish that I had read an experience like this before hand so that I knew it was possible. So that I wasn't afraid. So that I wasn't unprepared when I starting to have mild cramps and expected things to get much worse before anything happened. Because that's not what happened to me, it came quickly and painlessly when my body finally recognized the loss.

> Recently, my wife and I went through a heartbreaking premature labor at **21 weeks and 2 days** and lost our baby girl.  
> On **August 12, 2026**, we had our regular 20-week anatomy/anomaly scan. According to the report and doctor, both mom and baby looked normal, and we were not told about any major concerns.  
> Less than 24 hours later, on **August 13**, my wife started having abdominal pain and back pain. Later, we noticed blood when she urinated, so we went to the emergency room. After examination, the doctor confirmed that **preterm labor had already started and was progressing**.  
> Our baby girl was delivered on **August 14 at 2:32 AM**. She was born alive and fought for more than **3 hours**, but sadly she was declared deceased around **5:43 AM**.  
> We are still confused about how everything could change so quickly when the ultrasound looked normal just the day before. The doctors have not been able to give us a clear reason for why this happened.  
> Could this have been caused by **cervical insufficiency, infection, placental issues, membrane problems, contractions, or something that may not have been visible on the ultrasound**?  
> If anyone, especially an OB/GYN, MFM specialist, nurse, or parent who has experienced something similar, can share possible reasons or what tests/questions we should ask our doctor before a future pregnancy, we would really appreciate it.  
> We are not trying to blame anyone. We simply want to understand what may have happened. TIA. 🙏

Further supporting IDs: [pbu09-0303] [pbu34-0027] [pbu34-0028] [pbu34-0149] [pbu34-0222] [pbu34-0311] [pbu38-0011] [pbu38-0022] [pbu38-0050] [pbu38-0169] [pbu38-0260]


### B. Pregnancy number conceals living children, loss sequence and the experience a parent actually needs help with.


**Belongs in:** §2, second and later pregnancies; §9, segments; §10 For 3 and Against 5; §17.3.


**Relationship to the current findings: Extends and contradicts the simple gate.** Current conclusion: “Pregnancy number as a gate into her situation: holds.”


**What should change:** Keep pregnancy number only as an opening question. Add living children, prior miscarriage/ectopic/TFMR/stillbirth/neonatal loss, time since the loss, medical histories and what care or contact was missed. Fifth pregnancy can mean no children; a 'first baby' can follow losses; a second pregnancy after stillbirth does not mean previous newborn-care experience. This supports richer distinctions in research, not proof that a particular selector will sell.


Loss changes buying and disclosure as well as feelings: tests can feel like a jinx; announcements, names, showers and registries are postponed; gifts from a dead baby remain; parents fear returning or reimbursing gifts. A healthy living child does not erase fear in the next pregnancy. Wanting to celebrate after loss and wanting to keep the pregnancy private both appear.


**Verbatim evidence** (69 quote records assigned to this pattern; this is a coverage grouping, not a prevalence or independent-source count).

> Hii. So I’m about to be 9 months pregnant. In afew days. And I’m so nervous guys. I’m so scared. I had a loss before this. At 4 months. And another failed pregnancy. And I keep seeing posts about stillbirth. And how there were no signs, etc etc. it scares me because aside from these Posts, even before seeing them. I have had intrusive thoughts every single day about that happening to me. And I don’t know man. I know I overthink. But I’ve been putting off having a baby shower and registry for the longest. People have asked me. I didn’t even have a gender reveal because I was scared of losing the baby. And I feel bad for that. My partner didn’t mind but I don’t want to take these experiences away from him/us.  And then I’m like well what if I buy these things and have a baby shower and people buy things and everyone knows I’m pregnant, to then have a stillbirth! Like what do people even do in that situation. Having to tell everyone you lost a child is so hard and I don’t want to go thru that again, also what, I’ll have to give everyone’s presents back to them since they were a waste of money?? If the worst outcome were to happen.  So yeah. I’m just struggling here.


<a id="q-pbu34-0260"></a>


**[pbu34-0260]**

> I know that the dating could be a problem but honestly I think I'd rather not wait? that seems like the only downside and I'm like eventually the doctors could figure out the due date right? not seeing why my husband and I should wait to have sec or use birth control/condoms when we really want to be pregnan! miscarriage was hard but we have healed tremendously through our faith and support. will always be hard and of course a little scary, but can someone tell me their experienc? anyone has a pregnancy before first natural cycle and it's been fine? or vice versa


<a id="q-pbu34-0402"></a>


**[pbu34-0402]**

> I'm pregnant again and I can't allow myself to be excited or positive about the outcome. When people congratulate me I can only think about having to tell them about a loss later. I say things like "well I'm not sure, I'm high risk" and "don't count the chicken before it hatches". I can "plan" on what things will be like in a month because I might no longer be pregnant. 
> 
> I told myself I can believe it's real when I get a clean genetic test.  I have a 9 week ultrasound in 2 days and I'm already assuming it's bad news. I've been here before. 
> 
> I'm already planning my next steps like "I'll need a D&C and IUD because I'm closing up shop". Maybe I'll get on glp1s and lose this weight I keep gaining from going through a full pregnancy and 3 first trimesters. But I can't think of baby names. 


<a id="q-pbu34-0419"></a>


**[pbu34-0419]**

> This is my third pregnancy and I'm just so nervous its gonna happen again. I just found out two days ago and I'm almost at the seven week mark which is when I had my first miscarriage and I just feel really on edge. I'm slightly more faithful as the first and second time I had alot of bleeding and I haven't experienced this at all this time but I'm just alil scared that any day now I'm gonna go to the bathroom and see blood again. I'm trying to think positively and take it one step at a time but, its frustrating having to possibly be disappointed again and having to start all over from square one. The nausea, the appointments, the testing. I really want it to work out this time...


<a id="q-pbu34-0434"></a>


**[pbu34-0434]**

> I’m 49 years old, and expecting my (hopefully) first living child in 3 months (September). The pregnancy was very unexpectedly discovered at 16 weeks, during a gyn appointment, as I thought I had entered perimenopause and only previously had my IUD removed. I also thought I was infertile (previously 6 losses in my 20s, and stayed childfree by choice since, aside from a stepdaughter who I love as my own). Lots of consideration and mixed feelings, husband (late 30s) gave me the choice, and after originally choosing abortion, I couldn’t get myself through it and chose to keep it.


<a id="q-pbu38-0346"></a>


**[pbu38-0346]**

> We are pregnant with our 2nd, but our first was stillborn last year. I was thrown a baby shower for our first and I kept the few things that I was given then (along with what we were gifted from the registry). I want to have some sort of party whether that be a sprinkle or something like that.


<a id="q-pbu38-0360"></a>


**[pbu38-0360]**

> I’m at the point now where I made my registry and I ordered her nursery furniture and I can’t help but feel incredibly anxious about people purchasing me stuff. It’s expensive and thoughtful and I just feel guilty and can’t shake the idea that the rug could be pulled out from me any moment and then I’d need to find a way to get people’s money back for all this nice stuff.

### C. Reassurance often expires or creates a new uncertainty, but it can also give real relief.


**Belongs in:** §6, Waiting, reassurance, stories, search and AI; §10, information use.


**Relationship to the current findings: Confirms and extends.** Current conclusion: “More information can reassure or renew fear.”


**What should change:** Specify the loop: symptom loss or absence, test-line comparisons across brands, hCG measurements across laboratories, wearable temperature/heart-rate data, Dopplers and successive scans become additional things to interpret. Include the cost and injury caused by checking, delayed announcements, and the difference between a scan being too early to answer a question and an established loss. Do not turn every appropriate report of bleeding or pain into an anxiety diagnosis.


Parents often understand probabilities or the limits of control and still cannot feel reassured. One parent describes physically injuring herself while checking for blood. Others describe relief from a heartbeat, rising blood tests or an appropriate early placement scan. Their reported outcomes do not establish a medical rule about symptoms, test darkness or viability.


**Verbatim evidence** (141 quote records assigned to this pattern; this is a coverage grouping, not a prevalence or independent-source count).


<a id="q-pbu09-0013"></a>


**[pbu09-0013]**

> My mum didn’t hit peri until she was 50, and, I dunno, I just… think I’m pregnant. However with all of my early losses, I literally got a BFP and then started bleeding a day or two later and now I’ve just got myself into this horrible hole where I feel like taking the test is gonna jinx it 😭😭😭 my doctor messaged me urging me to find out as age + previous losses means I need prenatal monitoring ASAP but… I cannot. I just… cannot make myself POAS 😣


<a id="q-pbu09-0016"></a>


**[pbu09-0016]**

> I keep thinking that when I get to X point in the pregnancy then I’ll stop worrying, but it seems that the goalposts keep moving and now I worry not about having a miscarriage but about going to give birth and not coming home with a healthy baby.


<a id="q-pbu09-0019"></a>


**[pbu09-0019]**

> My first ultrasound isn’t for another week, and I’m struggling. Im supposedly around 7 weeks pregnant. I’ve had nausea and bloating every single day the last week, and then today, I feel completely totally fine. The Internet tells me that’s totally normal because your hormones fluctuate and this could just be a good day… but with my first pregnancy, I had all my symptoms totally stop, and asked to see my doctor because I thought it was weird. Turns out, I had a missed miscarriage.
> 
> Have you had your symptoms fluctuate like that (like have a whole day without symptoms) and have your baby end up totally fine? I think I’m looking for reassurance from women who actually have had such fluctuations and it didn’t mean anything terrible like it did for me the first pregnancy.
> 
> Any insight would be helpful as I hold on for my scan.


<a id="q-pbu09-0156"></a>


**[pbu09-0156]**

> I’ve probably made things worse by overanalyzing—comparing pregnancy test lines (newer ones maybe slightly lighter) and even looking at my Oura Ring data like HRV changes.
> 
> I had a prior miscarriage before my son, so this has been really triggering and hard not to spiral.
> 
> I’m seeing my OB on Monday for a scan and will be 7 weeks then, but getting through the weekend is feeling really emotionally taxing.


<a id="q-pbu09-0175"></a>


**[pbu09-0175]**

> It is relentless. The nightmares are graphic, gory, vivid and deeply upsetting. If they’d only just started, I would’ve linked it to the fact it has just been my son’s second remembrance (yesterday), but they have been going on for a while. I barely sleep. I don’t function. I’m currently off work with mental health issues (have been off since before I fell pregnant) and awaiting redeployment, so I can’t even just go back like I could with normal sickness. I need something to break this cycle and keep me sane, but I’m in the house whilst my son is at school and husband at work every day. 
> 
> I just want to cry. I told myself at 6 weeks I’d feel better at 7, and so on and so forth. The anxiety just keeps switching the goalposts. I am now resenting the fact I’m pregnant, because the mental load is too much. 
> 
> When I was around 5 weeks, I actually physically injured myself “down there” by accident from constantly wiping to check for blood. Ended up with a small laceration.


<a id="q-pbu09-0177"></a>


**[pbu09-0177]**

> With my first baby, I did SO much to prepare. Supplements, preconception labs, hours upon hours of podcasts and audiobooks, even seeing a fertility specialist from the start. I was on 7.5mg of letrozole and progesterone suppositories for my first. I still have the progesterone so I’ve been using those since I found out about a week ago.
> 
>  
> 
> I got beta hCG drawn. On Friday, it was 380 and it increased to 1320 by Monday. No cramps, no bleeding other than super light and super minimal spotting the day before I tested (which was about a week before the second hCG draw). I have an ultrasound scheduled for May 20 with a local pregnancy center and June 2 with my midwife. Those dates seem forever away. 
> 
> 
> 
> I feel no symptoms. With my first, I felt nauseous and exhausted but I can’t remember when that started. I am worried I didn’t prepare my body enough and will experience a MMC.


<a id="q-pbu09-0197"></a>


**[pbu09-0197]**

> I was so hoping that my ultrasound would bring me clarity—I’m actually just kind of feeling worse now.   
> I was supposedly 7 weeks pregnant based on last menstrual cycle, but my ultrasound dated me 4 weeks 5 days with a 0.8 sized gestational sac. (Abdominal ultrasound only, I didn’t do a vaginal).   
> A little background—I likely ovulated between May 1st and 4th, my body temp rose May 11th to 99.7 which I assumed may be implantation because my temp is usually much lower (I track daily) and my first faint positive was May 14th.   
> My hcg May 14th was 26.6   
> May 19th 240.5   
> May 21st 557.9   
> May 26th 2,772.7   
> May 28th 3,912.4  
> June 1st 8,036.4 
> 
> I’ve had just a drop of bleeding a few days ago. But otherwise just normal symptoms. Nausea, a pulling feeling in my abdomen, sore breasts, and today I started vomiting so I feel like that would mean hcg is rising? My OB called me and said I may be miscarrying. I’m just so confused. And upset. I know she thought I was 7 weeks, so this throws things off a bit, but I don’t feel like anything is wrong here?? So far all the signs seem good.

**[pbu09-0211]**

<a id="q-pbu09-0212"></a>


**[pbu09-0212]**

> I’m freaking out about the quest numbers. If I just compare the two labcorp numbers they are good but the quest number throws everything off. I don’t know what to think. I called my ob and she scheduled lab work for tomorrow (Wednesday) and Friday!


<a id="q-pbu09-0265"></a>


**[pbu09-0265]**

> * Line progression: My lines just didn't seem to be getting as dark as everyone else's. I tested obsessively and finally got my husband to hide the tests so I could quit thinking about it. I still thought about it obsessively, but we got through it and it didn't mean anything in the end.
> * hCG numbers: Similarly, I was on the lower end of the hCG numbers for all the posts I was seeing in my baby bumps group. My numbers did double on-time, however, and that's really all I should have paid attention to.
> * One-sided cramping and light bleeding: I was absolutely convinced for a few days that I must be having an ectopic. This one in particular you should get checked out if you have real worries! For me, it just turns out that I ovulated on my right side, and that's where most of the cramping ended up. I also spotted throughout the first trimester, although it was never heavy. 
> * Drop in heart rate: I wear an Apple Watch, which showed that my resting heart rate skyrocketed as soon as I got pregnant. Then at about 6 weeks it crashed back down to pre-pregnancy levels. I was so completely sure that must mean I was about to miscarry. Fortunately, it had nothing to do with it; I guess my body just adjusted to the higher blood flow.

**[pbu09-0306]**

I’m just tired of being up in the air, whether it’s a viable pregnancy, ectopic, or if I will miscarry eventually. Add into that stress, I’m a teacher at the beginning of the school year. I just want to know what’s happening inside my body y’all.


<a id="q-pbu09-0331"></a>

> I have to go back for another scan in 8 days, when I’ll be 6 weeks and 1 day, so hopefully there will be lots more to see and some lovely growth by then.

### D. Screening and fetal diagnoses create competing needs for certainty, preparation and a decision about continuing pregnancy.


**Belongs in:** §4, choices and explanations; §6, fear and information; §9, history; add prenatal-diagnosis and testing decisions.


**Relationship to the current findings: Extends.** Current conclusion: “The decision and care experience need their own treatment.”


**What should change:** Develop the presently thin diagnosis/testing account: screening versus diagnosis, uncertainty while waiting, questions for the next visit, future birth/surgery/daycare planning, and the relationship between findings and termination decisions. Some want amniocentesis and would TFMR; others decline testing because results would not change their choice or because of perceived procedure risk. Do not imply that more testing or less information is universally preferable.


Prior successful births can lead to choosing free screening; a subsequent high-risk result changes that calculation. Parents ask to reconcile differing reports and request experiences with uncommon findings. One clinic focuses on today's readings while the family needs to plan surgery and the end of paid leave. Normal later tests and growth after a frightening prognosis are retained as counter-evidence; prognosis, procedure-risk figures and causal interpretations remain attributed testimony.


**Verbatim evidence** (43 quote records assigned to this pattern; this is a coverage grouping, not a prevalence or independent-source count).


<a id="q-pbu09-0014"></a>


**[pbu09-0014]**

> I’m 33 years old with second baby on the way (16 weeks and 3 days) and I opted to do the SIPS test because it was the free option and I figured I have a child already and nothing went wrong with her. I received a call from my doctor yesterday saying I tested “positive” or “high risk” of our baby having Down syndrome with the odds being 1:100.


<a id="q-pbu09-0015"></a>


**[pbu09-0015]**

> I went into the hospital today to do the NIPT since I just want my results quicker and to have my mind at ease and the nurse doing my bloodwork was so irritated with the fact that so many women are doing these tests after so many high risk results. They believe it’s just a big money grab because it ends up being fine 9 times out of 10. I want to believe I’m fine but also be cautious. Has anyone around the same age had this happen to them because they opted to do the SIPS instead of pay for the NIPT first?


<a id="q-pbu09-0046"></a>


**[pbu09-0046]**

> An ultrasound at 19+3 weeks revealed a heart defect in the fetus. The heart is located on the left side, but its axis is shifted approximately 95 degrees to the right. There is a suspicion of a collision (displacement) of the aorta, alternatively - a variant of the common arterial trunk. Has anyone had such a diagnosis? I do not want an abortion. Is there a chance that the child will not die after birth? Now they have passed genetic tests.


<a id="q-pbu09-0060"></a>


**[pbu09-0060]**

> We tried to have a baby with ICSI and embryo selection but were unsuccessful. We then decided we wanted to do it the natural way, and if it was a boy we wouldn’t do the tests to determine the gene. (Because for us, the 8% more chance is a negligible risk). We have a baby boy now. We really want a sibling for him. But I don’t know if I can go through the uncertainty again… if I get pregnant, and it is a girl, we will do the genetic test (chorionic villus sampling) if she carries the gene, the rational decision would be abortion. Because we don’t want her to have to go through cancer (lots of cancers in his family, we know how hard it is). But I don’t know if I would be able to make that decision once I am pregnant. When I was pregnant with my son and didn’t know he was a boy yet, the hormones were raging and I felt I couldn’t give this baby up. Luckily I didn’t have to, but now I know how I felt while pregnant, and rationally decisions plus hormones are not a good match.


<a id="q-pbu09-0091"></a>


**[pbu09-0091]**

> I had my 20 week scan and was diagnosed with placenta accreta and they referred me urgently to fetal medicine to re-scan me to see how severe it is. 
> 
> Was given a leaflet and first thing I see is that most cases need a hysterectomy, something like over 65% and over 30% of women will be admitted to ICU after birth. Made the mistake of googling and saw the mortality rate of 7% and I’m so upset I can’t stop crying. 
> 
> I’m only 16. I have 7 sisters and always pictured many children for myself and now I’ll only have 1 if I survive this all. I don’t know what to do anymore. 
> 
> Not sure what I’m asking for online. My family are all panicking too so not the most calming environment.


<a id="q-pbu09-0145"></a>


**[pbu09-0145]**

> Hi all, I just was diagnosed with cholestasis (levels are 20 at 32/33 weeks). I have a long history (tw: third trimester stillbirth due to different reasons). I’m wondering whether anyone who’s had something similar in a previous pregnancy can tell me about their child — I’m worried about a newer study showing an increased risk of neurodevelopmental issues for babies whose mom had cholestasis in pregnancy.
> 
> I’m of course worried short term too but I’ve understood from my doctors how to manage this.


<a id="q-pbu09-0200"></a>


**[pbu09-0200]**

> Just had my ultrasound 9+1 today. It started off great, saw heartbeat and the tiny baby. I had an appt with my OBGYN after where the tone quickly changed. The ultrasound showed Pleural Effusion. They suspect it can be a genetic abnormality, but we’ll need to wait a week to do the genetic testing bloodwork. Me nor my husband are aware of any genetic abnormalities that run in our family. This is my 5th pregnancy, I’ve had 1 live birth. So the amount of miscarriages is definitely a concern. The doctor seems to think the pregnancy will not be viable. I’m just going to be in hell speculating over the next week. Does anyone have experience with this? I’d like to know, good or bad. I just want to be prepared as I can be.


<a id="q-pbu09-0226"></a>


**[pbu09-0226]**

> I has another followup today and although they assured me everything looked stable its hard not to spiral. I keep looking up the reading and studies out there but I am so worried about the baby developing hydrops and all the unkowns about delivery and when the baby will need surgery. The clinic seems to just focus on the readings that day vs. future but I want to know what to expect.
> 
> If you had a baby with this or similar diagnosis what was your experience? Were you ever diagnosed with fetal hydrops? Did you have to deliver early or via c-section or EXIT procedure? Did they attempt the steroid injections to help at any point? After delivery - when was your baby’s surgery and what was recovery like? By this time I was 25 weeks and baby was 600g at the 3rd percentile and the echogenic bowel had resolved. I was starting to have some hope for the first time in weeks. Things were looking stable and I kept praying to make it to at least 30 weeks. And I did, I made it to 30 and beyond. Baby kept growing, stayed at the 5-6th percentile at every growth scan with absolutely normal dopplers and I had normal blood pressure. I made it to 37 weeks at which point they induced me, which went very smooth and fast but this was my 4th baby so it was expected to go fast.   
> My little miracle and fighter, my baby girl, was born weighing 2241g at the 6th percentile. And after a very short NICU admission because of her blood sugar levels, she came home and she is finally safe in my arms.

Continued: [pbu34-0354] [pbu38-0236] [pbu38-0249] [pbu38-0280] [pbu38-0334] [pbu38-0340] [pbu38-0357]


### E. Whether to continue a pregnancy remains a live decision even when the baby was wanted or deliberately conceived.


**Belongs in:** §1, the reader; §2, early pregnancy; §6, wants and fears; §8, constraints; §10, scope; add pregnancy-decision and abortion experiences.


**Relationship to the current findings: Extends.** Current conclusion: “Trimester, pregnancy number and a calendar are not sufficient personalisation by themselves.”


**What should change:** Include a pregnancy-decision stage instead of presuming a settled decision to give birth. Wanting the baby can coexist with choosing or considering abortion because of money, housing, schooling, immigration, remembered postpartum illness, bodily suffering or partner behaviour. Agreement before conception can change afterwards. Distinguish the author's choice, uncertainty, religious or moral conflict, practical restriction and external coercion.


The words include unwanted pregnancy with a wanted baby, wishing for miscarriage to avoid making the decision, grief after an abortion, fear that either decision will bring regret, and a scan changing a decision toward continuation. A supportive partner or comfortable finances does not resolve ambivalence. Reports about local abortion access are the speakers' accounts, not independently checked law.


**Verbatim evidence** (75 quote records assigned to this pattern; this is a coverage grouping, not a prevalence or independent-source count).

> Last night, out of nowhere, my girlfriend told me she got a pregnancy test and she is pregnant. She told me this and told me she has no idea how she feels, but she wanted to talk to me first before coming to a conclusion on what to do. My initial reaction was, I don't really know what to do. I wasn't expecting this. I have a lot of concerns though. I don't know how we can do this, but I will support you no matter what we end up deciding. I gave her a list of concerns in regards to if we keep it, those concerns being financial, is our relationship healthy enough, we don't have family here to help us, etc. I listed concerns if she decides to get rid of it, and those concerns included how it would negatively affect her emotions, how it would negatively impact our relationship with each other, would she view it as a painful memory that she associates with me now, etc. She heard this, paused for a little while, became avoidant, and then told me she was extremely disappointed because after talking with me, she realized she had actually been more excited than she initially thought she was, and that she was very disappointed that I had not been as excited as she was, and that I had not been optimistic ("this is amazing! Lets figure out how we can make this work!"). To her credit, she's correct. I did not immediately jump for joy and leap around with enthusiasm. However, to my credit, I did not try to convince her to abort it. I didn't try to convince her to have it either. All I told her was this is a big decision, I have concerns both ways, and no matter what, please believe me when I say I will be here to support you through it. She took that as me not being excited to have this kid and have this life with her.

### F. Some partner failures are reproductive coercion, sexual coercion or violence, with practical barriers to leaving.


**Belongs in:** §5, partners; §6, boundaries; §9, support situations; §10 For 6; §12 and §17.7, historical gaps.


**Relationship to the current findings: Contradicts the narrow framing and extends the restoration.** Current conclusion: “the gap is mental load more than absence”


**What should change:** Replace that generalisation with distinct situations: willing but uninformed support; limited capacity; disengagement; and deliberate control, threats or violence. These records describe forced conception, punching or kneeing the pregnant abdomen, pressure both to abort and to continue, threats of suicide or abandonment, and repeated sexual touching or demands after refusal. A book, audio format or task list cannot explain or resolve those situations.


Keep the material barriers in view: housing belongs to the partner, money or transport is withheld, employment has ended, relatives are unavailable, or custody and legal bills are feared. Some authors still call the partner otherwise kind or reject the label 'abuse'; preserve their words rather than treating that as evidence the conduct did not happen. A partner's own account of regretting pressure for an abortion supplies the other side.


**Verbatim evidence** (52 quote records assigned to this pattern; this is a coverage grouping, not a prevalence or independent-source count).

I genuinely believe I could continue my degree and that, over the next three or four years, we could become financially stable while raising a child. He has also told me that he feels he can’t be vulnerable around me because he believes I took advantage of him when he was vulnerable during the situation with his dad, and that I should have been the one to refuse and take my birth control because I should have known better. I now feel extremely guilty and torn because I don’t want him to feel that I betrayed or took advantage of him, but at the same time, I don’t want to have an abortion when I want to continue the pregnancy. I’m struggling to understand how much responsibility I should take for what happened, whether I have done something morally wrong, and how to handle the situation with him while respecting his feelings without giving up my own wishes. Continued: [pbu38-0172] [pbu38-0174] [pbu38-0183] [pbu38-0243] [pbu38-0345] [pbu38-0356]


### G. A wanted pregnancy can bring severe depression, suicidal thoughts, intrusive fears, numbness and shame despite support.


**Belongs in:** §2, functioning; §6, Fear, shame, self-blame, identity and unwanted expectations; §9, previous mental-health experience.


**Relationship to the current findings: Extends.** Current conclusion: “The restored words include panic, sadness, lack of excitement, body distress, regret and fear of not bonding or surviving birth.”


**What should change:** Name the severity and mechanisms rather than compressing them into ordinary worry: passive or active suicidal language, intrusive fears of harming a child, loss of function, antenatal recurrence of previously treated illness, disgust or disconnection, and fear of repeating childhood abuse. Distinguish unwanted intrusive thoughts from reported actions; the toddler-discipline passage is retained under the newborn/capacity pattern. Neither planned conception nor a supportive spouse guarantees excitement or a bond.


Several parents fear that disclosing the extent of distress will produce an involuntary hold, social-care involvement or judgment. Others struggle to begin care, feel hurt by referral, or cannot afford it. The section includes gender disappointment and fear about raising a son without diagnosing the author from the quote. Later improvement and the purchases that briefly make a baby feel real are counter-examples to a uniformly worsening story.


**Verbatim evidence** (59 quote records assigned to this pattern; this is a coverage grouping, not a prevalence or independent-source count).

### H. Medication choices can force a trade between functioning, tolerating treatment and fear of harming the baby.


**Belongs in:** §2, symptoms; §5, care communication; §6, guilt; §8, Illness, treatment, work and leave interact.


**Relationship to the current findings: Extends.** Current conclusion: “The leave puzzle also involves being too ill to function, medication that interferes with work, notes withheld, paid leave spent before birth, and lost pay or insurance.”


**What should change:** Add continuity before the first OB appointment: abrupt or clinician-directed medication changes, refill interruptions, withdrawal, treatment that no longer seems effective, vomiting tablets, sedation and uncertainty about restarting. Include breastfeeding plans that conflict with needed postpartum medication, and explicit requests for a reasoned risk discussion rather than 'they would not prescribe it if unsafe'. These are accounts of decisions and beliefs, not treatment recommendations.


Parents describe ADHD medication versus school/work performance, switching to energy drinks, nausea relief versus alertness while caring for children, and previous opioid-related impaction limiting labour analgesia. Some report benefit from treatment; others describe feeling unusually well after stopping medication with clinicians aware. Retain both without turning either into advice or an outcome guarantee. Reported worries about fetal development, preeclampsia or sexual orientation remain their worries, not established effects.


**Verbatim evidence** (66 quote records assigned to this pattern; this is a coverage grouping, not a prevalence or independent-source count).

### I. Pregnancy can remove the substance that served a coping or earning function before another source of support exists.


**Belongs in:** §2, functioning; §5, reliable support; §6, shame and disclosure; §8, access; add sobriety, substance use and postpartum recovery.


**Relationship to the current findings: Extends into a missing subject.** Current conclusion: “What is missing from the advice she gets is *when* each thing applies to her and what to do if it doesn't.”


**What should change:** Develop substance use and recovery rather than leaving it among generic exposure fears. Include what alcohol, nicotine, cannabis or cocaine was doing for the author; attempts to stop; withdrawal affecting driving and earnings; guilt; disclosure fears; sober support during pregnancy; and fear that the protective motivation will weaken after birth. Partner or relative intoxication also changes who can be trusted to help.


One author describes trafficking, sex work, alcohol for trauma/anxiety and cocaine for staying awake to earn, then explicitly asks for truthful, non-cruel guidance and replacement supports. Another reports cannabis relieving otherwise unmanageable HG after costly failed treatments and a booked abortion; the accompanying statement that she is not recommending it is reproduced too. AA absence caused by symptoms and long shifts is followed by a welcoming return. These do not establish treatment safety or effectiveness.


**Verbatim evidence** (30 quote records assigned to this pattern; this is a coverage grouping, not a prevalence or independent-source count).


<a id="q-pbu09-0107"></a>


**[pbu09-0107]**

> Since finding out I was pregnant, I've been sober from both alcohol and marijuana for over 7 months. Staying sober while pregnant has honestly not been difficult because protecting my baby is non-negotiable to me.
> 
> What has been difficult is realizing that all the reasons I drank are still here.
> 
> In these past 7 months, I've probably left my house willingly fewer than 10 times outside of prenatal appointments. I don't even like going into my backyard because I hate feeling seen or perceived. Alcohol used to make that fear disappear.
> 
> I'm scared that once my baby is born, I'll lose the one thing that has kept me completely sober. I don't miss being drunk. I miss the relief I felt from my own mind.
> 
> Has anyone else experienced this fear during pregnancy? If you stayed sober postpartum, especially if you were using alcohol to self-medicate anxiety or other mental health issues, what helped you get through it?


<a id="q-pbu09-0108"></a>


**[pbu09-0108]**

I've tried multiple SSRIs and SNRIs, some for well over a year, and unfortunately none of them made any difference for me. I've also tried hypnotherapy. The only medication that noticeably helped was Vyvanse, which I stopped before trying to conceive.

> Fast forward to now. I’m married with my own family and I am very very happy with my life except for my mother. I finally have my first baby on the way. My mother has been obsessive about me having this baby but has done nothing to help prepare for it. She would offer to help with baby shower and then helped zero percent. She came  
> to my baby shower and walked to the corner store and bought wine and champagne and came back and got drunk with it. Everyone saw. She drove home after. She continues to drink most days. Probably more. When I call her out on it she acts like I’m dramatizing bc I am pregnant. She “only drinks wine she’s not drunk on liquor.” All the denial. 
> 
> I am thinking about cutting her out of my life at this point but I wonder if that is cruel. I feel like I need to for my step son and my baby otw. She is hoping to meet the baby in the hospital but I really just can’t stomach the thought of her around. I am worried the guilt of cutting her out will be very heavy on me  
> post partum. Does anyone have a similar situation? Is it better to just cut them out? Or does the guilt never ease up? I am a very empathetic person but I feel so protective of my kids that I am strongly thinking about going no contact. Any advice is welcome.

<a id="q-pbu09-0344"></a>


**[pbu09-0344]**

> I got severe hypremesis gravidarum at 6.5 weeks and was hospitalized multiple times. I tried medications ranging from doxylemene to zofran to prednisone, hundreds and hundreds of dollars spent trying to get to vomiting to stop. I’m talking daily vomiting every 20 minutes, this resulted in me losing around 30 pounds within a month.   
> Blood tests showed I lacked nearly every nutrient needed in order to have a healthy pregnancy, my doctors had prescribed everything they could. I tried EVERYTHING I could. The doctors said baby would take everything she needed from my body, and that was true. I got the the point where i couldn’t walk on my own, my breathing was messed up, I was emaciated.   
> At 10 weeks, I booked the abortion appointment for a very wanted pregnancy. I was heartbroken but I was quite literally dying, and nothing was helping.   
> I asked my doctor if there was ANYTHING I could do or take to fix this. She said some mothers use cannabis products, but that she cannot recommend them as there’s not enough information regarding the effects for a doctor to recommend it as treatment.   
> So I tried it, and it worked. I could eat, I could drink, the vomiting eventually stopped and I was putting weight on. Smoking anything during pregnancy carries a risk of preterm/early birth along with suspected complications such as ADHD; so I chose to go with the route of low thc edibles and oils in severe moderation.   
> I want to clarify, I understand that I’m risking my babies health in some shape or form, but I was at the point in my pregnancy that my doctors were unsure what more could be done. The conversations at the hospital went from “we can try this medication” to “your health is the most importantly factor”.   
> I am not proud that this is what’s helped me, and if I could have it be any other medication, I would. if I could have it be any other way, I would. I did not want to abort my child, and I did not want to die.

### J. Pregnancy symptoms can remove basic physical capacity, and generic encouragement or additional housework cannot restore it.


**Belongs in:** §2, Symptoms, attempts at relief, and the cost of functioning; §7, stock advice; §9, disability and older-child care; §10, weekly content.


**Relationship to the current findings: Confirms and extends.** Current conclusion: “These accounts sharpen the earlier complaint about expectations into a problem of daily function; women report benefits as well as failed attempts.”


**What should change:** Show the actual tasks lost: standing, walking, eating, sleeping, using hands, concentrating, caring for another child, getting out of bed or tolerating a commute. Include disability-specific constraints and compounded conditions. Severe nausea can influence whether a wanted pregnancy continues, so it cannot be treated solely as a coping or week-by-week expectation problem. Repeated failed remedies are evidence of attempts, not a reason to repeat the same suggestions.


There are reports of urinary retention, constipation requiring hospital treatment, faintness, pain despite belts/braces, infections and breathing problems. One disabled author says walking makes pain worse for the following day and fears not being able to lift the newborn. Others improve at twelve weeks or feel healthiest while pregnant. Even a household doing the chores can leave the pregnant parent feeling guilty or deprived of autonomy.


**Verbatim evidence** (71 quote records assigned to this pattern; this is a coverage grouping, not a prevalence or independent-source count).

### K. Changed birth preferences, inadequate pain relief and violations of consent are different experiences.


**Belongs in:** §4, Birth preferences and Consent; §6, birth fear; §10, birth choices and support.


**Relationship to the current findings: Confirms and extends.** Current conclusion: “Flexibility alone does not explain a good or difficult birth.”


**What should change:** Retain the difference between choosing an intervention as circumstances change and an intervention performed without asking or despite an explicit refusal. Include cervical stretching announced afterwards, an episiotomy despite saying no, a reported medication given without consent, and information withheld from the patient but discussed with her husband. Preparation includes how to communicate prior trauma without fearing worse treatment.


Choice runs in opposite directions: a CSA survivor explicitly wants surgery; others fear surgery, numbness or needles; another wants an advance conditional plan and relief from repeated decisions. A mental-health birth plan with a private room and partner access still fails to secure sleep during induction. Pain history, rapid previous births, hospital distance, blood-thinner timing and an abuser's birthday change the plan. Outcome and care experience must remain separate.


**Verbatim evidence** (54 quote records assigned to this pattern; this is a coverage grouping, not a prevalence or independent-source count).

She then listed all the negative outcomes from having C-sections and told me that I could most likely easily avoid that. When I asked about increased risks of complications during vaginal delivery after having had a C-section she did say there was increased risks for certain things like a placental rupture but that they are low and could happen any time in the 3rd trimester and that they monitor all that and have the OR ready just in case anyway. 
>

Finally a nurse noticed my fundal height increasing and my catheter not producing urine. My room once again flooded with nurses. Anesthesia came after an hour of agony and my incision getting pulled apart. I was getting prepped for surgery to inflate a balloon to stop the bleeding. A veteran nurse waited for the meds to take effect and manually extracted the clots and used her full body weight to do fundal massage. The bleeding stopped. I fortunately avoided surgery. Hematology explained to me that I was in DIC and needed a lot of bags of blood and clotting factors to stabilize me. I spent the remaining time in the hospital shaking and afraid of anyone touching my body. Moving felt like irons being held onto my stomach.

> My experience with the hospital during labour triggered significant mental health issues, including severe depression, anxiety and PTSD. I was eventually diagnosed with these conditions, and continue to receive therapy and medication with the support of a psychologist and a psychiatrist. I am still prone to flashbacks and I have a list of triggers to avoid. I am lucky to have support, but I do feel sad about the cost. All my treatment has had to be privately funded and this is NOT cheap!! We have had to spend thousands of pounds, and this is a lot for our family.


<a id="q-pbu34-0152"></a>


**[pbu34-0152]**

> Trust me when I say, it was a very calm atmosphere in the house, but that rapidly changed when my wife started screaming, actually screaming, “THE BABY IS COMING!”  I had heard of these stories, but never did I think we would ever find ourselves in this situation.  I was downstairs and she upstairs, so I ran up as fast as I humanly could and quickly concluded that there was no way we were getting to the hospital in time.  This was going down right here, right now.  I had her stand in the bathtub while I called for an ambulance. One minute later she was pushing and the baby started coming.  Having witnessed the birth of my first three children, I felt relatively calm and in control. I figured all I had to do was catch that baby and wait for the ambulance. But things are never that simple. First, the baby crowned and her head came completely out. But then that was it. She couldn’t get any farther. My wife is screaming, pushing, but all I could see was the baby’s face, blue and motionless.  How long could she stay like that and be fine, I thought.  After another minute of pushing she came out, and I caught her and immediately brought her up to my wife’s arms, who was standing during this whole ordeal. I was completely relieved but only for a moment when we realized that baby wasn’t crying or breathing. We first started stimulating her on her back then her front, back and forth, frantically doing whatever we could think of, but there was no response. Baby was blueberry blue and motionless. A minute passed of this, and we noticed just a tiny reaction from her, as if she was trying to breathe but couldn’t. We both said we had to start sucking stuff out of her chest. My mind immediately went toward scrambling to find a bulb sucker. But thank God for motherly instincts because my wife started immediately sucking stuff out from baby’s mouth with her own mouth. And amazingly within seconds we got a small response, like a little cough. Then a few seconds more of sucking and we got a bigger cough. And shortly thereafter the first breath and the first cry. Baby immediately turned red; it was amazing. By this time it must have been at least two minutes since the birth, but it most definitely felt like an eternity. A few minutes after that baby let out the first cry, the medics arrived, and they took amazing care of her after that.  Eventually they were transferred to the nearby hospital.  Ironically, where we live, home births are outlawed.  The baby’s birth certificate reads that she was born in the hospital.

### L. Induction, epidural, surgery, ECV and practical support can bring relief, pride and a positive experience.


**Belongs in:** §4, attempts and outcomes; §5, support that works; §16.4 and §17.6, contrary evidence.


**Relationship to the current findings: Confirms.** Current conclusion: “Some find relief, confidence and an experience better than they feared.”


**What should change:** Keep positive medical and preparation accounts integrated with difficult accounts. A changed plan or assistance does not by itself mean trauma. Preserve gratitude for interventions, effective pain relief, consent during ECV or dose changes, uncomplicated early parenting, and helpful free peer groups. Do not infer that flexibility, a method or buying a guide caused the outcome.


Some still need time to process a lost birth plan while being grateful for surgery. Positive surgery can coexist with later NICU separation. Hands-on learning can teach more than classes, and paid preparation does not eliminate fear. These are counter-examples to deterministic narratives in either direction.


**Verbatim evidence** (22 quote records assigned to this pattern; this is a coverage grouping, not a prevalence or independent-source count).

Further supporting IDs: [pbu09-0238] [pbu09-0240] [pbu34-0313] [pbu34-0314] [pbu38-0085]


### M. Recovery and feeding involve competing needs, physical incapacity and grief that can last beyond the first two weeks.


**Belongs in:** §2, Recovery, feeding, sleep and bonding; §4, postpartum preparation; §10 For 5; §17.1, beyond two weeks.


**Relationship to the current findings: Confirms and extends.** Current conclusion: “A healthy baby or an outwardly successful birth does not settle what the mother experienced; relief and improvement also appear.”


**What should change:** Make recovery functional: inability to stand for a diaper change, sit through a feed, sleep in the bed, obtain rest or manage readmission while caring for a toddler. Include sedation versus alertness, infection and bleeding, painful sex and long-term scars. Feeding accounts need room for triple-feeding overload, exclusive-pumping regret, formula relief, pressure and guilt, and the need to change plans when medication or trauma makes nursing difficult.


One parent trades pain relief for wakefulness after her baby falls when she unintentionally falls asleep. Another links formula guilt to staff messages about SIDS; that is the meaning she reports, not a clinical claim. Parents describe not feeling worth caring for and stopping medication. Later accounts are included where they explicitly trace the effects of birth and the first weeks, not as evidence that every later parenting topic belongs in the build.


**Verbatim evidence** (31 quote records assigned to this pattern; this is a coverage grouping, not a prevalence or independent-source count).

> We had her 1st pediatrician appointment and she had lost about 7%. We went back two days later and she was down to 9%. I felt like my milk came in on this day so we kept trying to EBF - doctor recommended I pump and just supplement her with .5 ounce of BM and bam she started gaining. I went back to EBF and she was gaining but not as much as expected. I went to two lactation groups and they did weighted feeds and she was pulling milk but also not enough. They said that she was so sleepy because she wasn’t back to birthweight. This terrified me! Was my baby starving and couldn’t even manage staying awake?! They recommended triple feeding and we did so for two days. I believe after that she was back to birthweight. Somewhere during this the doctor also suggested I just pump for a full day and bottle feed her so we can get an idea of exactly how much milk I am producing and whether it was enough. This is where I feel like I lost control. 1st of all my husband and I couldn’t continue triple feeding. I wish I pushed more to just continue but with this being our first baby we were overwhelmed and it just felt like too much. We continued with “kinda” triple feeding when we could and then pumping. Long story long I couldn’t understand how to transition back to exclusively nursing

Now my sweet perfect angel baby girl is 9 1/2 months and I am sitting here crying still not fucking able to get over not being able to nurse her and questioning whether I gave up too soon. I even am questioning if I stopped pumping too soon even though that was so difficult (especially as a firewife). So anyways that’s my word vomit. ### N. Separation, disrupted learning and sleep loss can transform the first weeks even after a birth described as good.


**Belongs in:** §2, first weeks; §5, partner and help; §6, bond and fear; §10, postpartum section.


**Relationship to the current findings: Extends.** Current conclusion: “Make the first two weeks a full section: feeding, sleep, recovery supplies, the partner's shifts, visitors.”


**What should change:** Add the baby being taken away without explanation, the parent being unable to visit because of her own treatment, transfer transport without the parents, and missing the first week's hands-on learning. A later pregnancy can therefore bring first-time newborn-care fears. Extend the support horizon beyond discharge and beyond the date help or leave ends.


Sleep is not always restored by a sleeping baby or an allocated shift: anxiety, pain, ward conditions, feeding and inability to trust another caregiver can still prevent it. Colic, regret and absent instant bonding appear alongside an attentive father. Environmental constraints include a hot room, pets and smoke outside. The account of slapping/spanking a toddler in rage at five weeks postpartum is retained explicitly, together with unaffordable therapy, chatbot reliance and drinking to cope; these are connected pressures, not a claim about all parents.


**Verbatim evidence** (37 quote records assigned to this pattern; this is a coverage grouping, not a prevalence or independent-source count).

### O. Partner support depends on willingness, health, capacity and shared understanding, not only format or instructions.


**Belongs in:** §5, partners' own voices; §9, segments; §10 For 6; §17.1, format limit.


**Relationship to the current findings: Extends and qualifies.** Current conclusion: “Give the partner a short task list in a non-reading format”


**What should change:** Retain task-based material as one possible aid, while separating inability, disagreement and refusal. A partner may have epilepsy, recent cancer surgery, insomnia, depression, grief, a demanding job, little leave, distance or immigration restrictions. One parent already wrote an explicit emotional-support checklist and still received 'space' instead. More practical help can coexist with lost freedom, feeling misunderstood or unresolved relationship injury.


Fathers ask for help, describe loss and birth trauma, or worry their own mental-health work steals family time. A partner describes leaving the relationship while offering coparenting; another explains that neutral discussion of both pregnancy options was received as rejection. These accounts prevent treating every disagreement as identical coercion, without minimising the coercion described elsewhere. Shift work and daytime help sometimes do work.


**Verbatim evidence** (64 quote records assigned to this pattern; this is a coverage grouping, not a prevalence or independent-source count).


<a id="q-pbu09-0059"></a>


**[pbu09-0059]**

> Tonight I lay in bed crying while holding our 5.5 week old daughter thinking about how you can just leave us for the weekend and go to your friends party and drink and be merry while I stay at home with her because at this point I don’t even know if you’re helping. My mind is going to some dark ass places and I’m over here thinking how her and I can survive without you. Mostly how can I sleep. That may sound harsh, but the complete bare minimum that you’re doing as a father is surprising to me and has me feeling alone. 
> 
> Not to mention that when you’re with her (and she’s awake) you are either completely disconnected (ie: sleeping, on your phone, spacing out, watching tv or playing games) OR you’re trying to get her to go to sleep because you basically don’t seem to want to deal with her or even be with her. I can’t change that in you. And it makes me want to take her all the time but I need sleep and have to leave her sometimes to be able to do that.

**[pbu09-0065]**

> My wife lives with very complex and traumatic PTSD from an aggressively abusive upbringing. Over the years she has made progress. Her pregnancy seems to have undone some of the progress, though. She is incredibly depressed. She does the bare minimum in every aspect of her life. She hates everything. Everything hurts. She doesn't do any of the exercise her doctors have told her to do. If I hear her say "I don't care" one more time I might cry. Today she didn't eat anything until late in the afternoon when I got her some fast food (which we both rarely eat!). I try to gently talk to her because I see all the symptoms but she gets angry or deflects. 
> 
> I know she's pregnant. I know the hormones are a wild ride. But as the person who is in love with her and hurts when she hurts, how can I help guide her out of this? What can I say? What can I do? It is hard for me to watch her not love herself when she is growing our only child.

I have severe morning sickness and have had to go to the ER on multiple occasions and was planning on leaving my job but cant because I've been the one financially responsible and we have a baby on the way so we need money. I've been having discrimination and retaliation issues with my boss at work because of my pregnancy related absences at work that I'm working with HR on aswell. I just feel like everything bad that could be happening started happening when I got pregnant. Im so grateful for this baby and am so grateful we made it this far into this pregnancy but this is just too much to handle.

**[pbu09-0318]**

> My girlfriend is 6 weeks pregnant, and we have recurring mental health struggles. She is very attentive to her pregnancy symptoms, to the point that whenever she doesn’t have any, she starts to panic.
> 
> Since the beginning, I’ve been trying to reassure her by explaining that a decrease or absence of symptoms doesn’t necessarily mean anything about the baby’s health. But the more we move forward and the more I hear her worry about it, the more I’m starting to believe it myself, and I’m absolutely terrified.
> 
> I’m barely sleeping anymore. My girlfriend wakes up crying, and now I’m struggling to believe that everything is okay too. I’m scared of miscarriage, and this fear keeps getting stronger.
> 
> Have any of you experienced something similar? How did it turn out?  
> Do we have a reason to be concerned if her symptoms have decreased over the past two days?  
> How did you manage to get through this anxiety and fear?


<a id="q-pbu09-0341"></a>


**[pbu09-0341]**

> I have a good husband, truly. He’s doing almost all the cooking and cleaning, getting things that usually help me feel better etc. He’s doing all the right things. 
> 
> But he just doesn’t get it. He is CONSTANTLY asking me what’s wrong, what’s going on with me, if I’m okay etc whenever I’m acting tired or cranky or nauseous and acting confused about why I don’t wanna fill every day off with activities.
> 
> We go to the grocery store and I’m trying to go quickly, not making small talk, with what I’m sure is a huge RBF and he’s like “what’s wrong why are you acting this way?” My dude. I’m trying to get in and outta this store without puking right here in the chip aisle. 
> 
> We go to the park and I sit down on a bench for a bit and he’ll come over and ask why I’m not running around with them. My guy. I don’t feel well. 
> 
> I don’t know how to get through to him that for now, this is my constant of being. We’ve talked about it so many times. I feel like I’m speaking a different language.

**[pbu34-0083]**

I do the 11:30 p.m. and 6:00 a.m. feedings precisely to help her rest, but her sleep is still very fragmented.

>

Continued: [pbu38-0327] [pbu38-0343]


### P. Family access is a question of bodily privacy, trust and grief as well as a visitor calendar.


**Belongs in:** §3, stock lines; §5, relatives; §6, visitors; §10 For 7.


**Relationship to the current findings: Extends.** Current conclusion: “The visitor calendar alone misses bodily privacy, trust and the mother’s ability to recover.”


**What should change:** Add abusive family histories, actual threats or shoving, pressure to let unsafe relatives meet the baby, and the longing for a mother figure even when contact is harmful. Chosen support at a medical appointment or first night is not interchangeable with any available relative. Material help, expensive gifts or buying the largest registry item does not erase boundary problems.


Loss complicates announcements in both directions: parents protect a bereaved friend or relative while losing support for their own pregnancy, or hide pregnancy after being shamed for a miscarriage. Family members may schedule care without permission, claim access to the newborn, spread donor-conception details, or make grief about themselves. Useful listening, meals and boundary defence are counter-evidence and remain visible.


**Verbatim evidence** (81 quote records assigned to this pattern; this is a coverage grouping, not a prevalence or independent-source count).


<a id="q-pbu09-0031"></a>


**[pbu09-0031]**

> They told me I can not use the microwave anymore because of radiation and can not drink from plastic bottles because the baby will absorb all the microplastics at birth. (I wish I was making this up).
> 
> Even 2-3 weeks his mom asks me how far along I am despite us tell her the due date was in April after the first sonogram appt. Then asked me a few weeks later if I was 3 months pregnant yet (also made me uncomfortable). Told me that after I was 12 weeks my morning sickness would go away since that is when is does for most people, after me telling them I have HG. 17 weeks and still have terrible morning sickness and nausea.
> 
> His mom said she was going to set up a nursery in her and her husband's house so when the baby arrives we can stay there for the first few weeks (we literally live one street over), I didn't even say anything because I did not want her to think that would be happening.
> 
> They also said at one point that they "can't wait to take their grandchild to Europe for a summer", didn't even ask me if that was okay with me or their son. They have also called me the vessel of their grandchild that also made me feel uncomfortable.


<a id="q-pbu09-0032"></a>


**[pbu09-0032]**

> Then I found out that I was pregnant in August of this year and their demeanor immediately switched. They were immediately overjoyed and this is where things in my opinion got to be weird. The day we told his parents was the day we found out, around 6 weeks. That day she starts asking me which doctor I am going to and to call her doctor to setup an appointment, we said no and that we will find a doctor we like on our own. About two hours later when we went back home for like 20 minutes I get a call from his mom's doctor saying my MIL called to setup an appointment for me for that Friday, I said to cancel it as I did not setup this appointment.


<a id="q-pbu09-0048"></a>


**[pbu09-0048]**

> She is pregnant again and this one seems to be going very well but I am also mainly supporting her through this, being very careful to not speak much about my own pregnancy since I'm well into the "safety zone". She isn't requesting any of this from me but I would feel terrible if she loses this baby again and I go on to have mine in a few months. I think subconsciously I am not letting myself feel too attached or too excited because I got pregnant so quickly. I feel bad like its unfair to her that she has to struggle. My marriage is also rock solid and my husband can provide enough to stay home once baby is here. Her situation is different and I feel like I am "too blessed" to share or complain. 
> 
> The hardest part is I've been suffering with perinatal anxiety and depression severely... Its hard to even mention it because of how easy my pregnancy is going and how truthfully I have little to worry about compared to her. I am seeking treatment for it and doing my best to care for my mental health before baby comes.


<a id="q-pbu09-0072"></a>


**[pbu09-0072]**

> This post was inspired by the phone call we had today. She's terrified to give birth. She's already saying she changed her mind and doesn't want to do it. She was just sobbing and pouring out her heart to me. She said she thought her water broke (she got checked, it didn't) and that finally made it feel real. And now she's freaked. 
> 
> I wish I could do it for her. I don't want her to experience that pain or the fear. I don't want her to experience what it feels like for your vagina to be held together by stitches and it being painful to sit and feeling like it's going to fall out when you stand. I don't want her to experience that first postpartum poop. I don't want her to experience the baby blues, the anxiety, or the sleep deprivation, and everything that comes along with those things. I don't want her to hate her body. Like I said, she's been through so much in her life already, I just want to protect her. 
> 
> When she's asked me questions I made sure to be honest, but I definitely keep reminding her if the good parts and put an emphasis on them. We live states away from each other so unfortunately I can't be there physically, but I am trying to be as much help as possible. I plan on ordering their groceries to be delivered once baby gets here, and she has my Shipt account info with my card on there to get anything she needs delivered. But I wish I could be there to physically help her.


<a id="q-pbu09-0133"></a>


**[pbu09-0133]**

> 5 years ago when I was 23, I told my mom that I had a miscarriage. Her response wasn’t comforting at all. She instead shamed me and said “how could you even think to get pregnant before marriage.” She was beyond disgusted with me. Now, I am 28 and married and she is asking me every day to be a grandma… I really don’t want to tell her that I am pregnant or in labor.


<a id="q-pbu09-0202"></a>


**[pbu09-0202]**

> I (32F) just found out that I’m pregnant with my 3rd child after 2.5 years of unexplained secondary infertility (including 6 pregnancy losses). My other 2 kids are 3y and 5y. My dream has always been to be a mother and to have a big family (3-5 kids, but after every I just went through, 3 is perfectly fine and I’m extremely grateful). My family has never been on board with me being a mother. No particular reason, but probably because I am a type 1 diabetic and have been since I was 3 years old. My pregnancies have been a bit difficult towards the end, but both of my kids are perfectly healthy and thriving. I am regularly monitored by multiple doctors and have maintained good health in general. When my second child was born, we had some complications and she needed a longer nice stay and a feeding tube (g-tube) surgically attached. Long story short, she ended up not needing it long and no longer has it… as it turns out, my OB put me on a medication that caused her to be sedated and would be continually sedated as I kept breastfeeding her while taking the medication (no, I did not sue because the findings came almost a year later… I did not have money for a lawyer and was advised that this case could be difficult to win). I have explained to my family what happened but they still have the same feelings: that I shouldn’t have any more babies.


<a id="q-pbu09-0241"></a>


**[pbu09-0241]**

> I am 7 weeks today. This is my third pregnancy after two losses due to APLA. I have told only my parents and husband's parents. No one else knows. But my cousins and relatives are calling me saying that they are coming to my place this weekend to just visit me and my husband. I don't know how to avoid them. After they called I cried a lot because of fear. I can't tell them and I can't simply put on an act...because I am a high risk pregnancy and I am on bed rest till the first trimester ends. I am so scared about the intense crying. I just want my baby to be born healthy. I am hiding it from them because after my last miscarriage I was given lectures and unsolicited advice from them. I have heard so many hurtful things. They keep asking when I am going to announce the 'good news' for the past one year when I was struggling with secondary infertility. They have asked me questions that pierced my soul and I will never forget. I wish to tell no other relatives or friends till birth...till I see my healthy rainbow baby alive and well. How do I tackle all my relatives and friends?


<a id="q-pbu09-0300"></a>


**[pbu09-0300]**

> I met my husband and he healed me in so many ways. This man is the softest person I know and I'm so grateful to have him as family. Now I'm pregnant through a sperm donor because my husband is tr'ns, and my mom is like so weird about it??? The second she knew I was pregnant she got so nosey about how I got pregnant, asked for details about the actual sperm donation and our process. Then she asked heavily for a picture of the donor??? Then called back, said she told everything to my 85-years-old grandma, who is dead anxious and I need to do something about it. Like ma'am what did you tell her? Then, she shared the most traumatic shit ever about how she got mistreated by my dad when she delivered us. Just to tell me how beautiful it is to be pregnant and how she loved everything about it. Like lady, you told me numerous times in my childhood that having kids (me!!) was the worst mistake of your life. After that, she bought the most expensive thing from our baby list, got mad because I sent her a picture of the box and not the whole thing unboxed. Then she insisted to know the sex of the baby although I expressely told her I don't want people to know. Also, my house is apparently too small, I'm too poor and I don't understand how hard it is to care for a baby, I should get rid of my cats, I should move to live closer to them (hell no). Today I learned she's coming over tomorrow, with my dad?? Like wtf. I've had my own place for 13 years and she visited like 3 times.
> 
> Ok my rant is over. Thanks for reading my book. Why are people so weird when you're pregnant??


<a id="q-pbu09-0302"></a>


**[pbu09-0302]**

> I really don’t want to tell anyone yet. We haven’t even told our parents, and ideally we’d like them to know before our friends do. And obviously, the LAST thing I want is for people to figure it out at my friend’s wedding and have my pregnancy become a topic of conversation on her weekend.

>

>

The nurse said it was up to me, I was thrown of gard and didn't have the words to nicely say no, so I said sure. The Dr. came in and gave us the awful news that the pregnancy was ectopic. I lost it, feeling no comfort from my husband, I shucked up my tears and stuffed all my emotions. I know 100% he wasn't available to comfort me because his mom was in the room.

<a id="q-pbu34-0078"></a>


**[pbu34-0078]**

> I found out in January that we were expecting and then in February I had a miscarriage. We weren’t sure what was happening so we dropped our daughter off at in laws and went to the hospital. They left for vacation a few days later and 2 weeks after miscarriage visited us at home to bring gifts for our daughter. My MIL said “she needs a sibling” my husband and I both didn’t know wtf to say so we ignored. After when we talked about it my husband cried, because how could she be so insensitive?


<a id="q-pbu34-0085"></a>
